Sir:
72
May I refer to my letter of 31 December 1929,
relative to the interference between Iwaki and cavite?
I have recently received two letters from the
Traffio Engineer of the Radio Corporation which indicate
that the frequency of Iwaki has been temporarily shifted
with very satisfactory results. I am attaching herewith
copies of these letters with the request that the State
Department endeavor to further the permanence of this
proposed change to l9.6 kilocycles.
Respectfully,
At ng Seoretary or the Navy

=== Page 2 === (confidence 0.99)
R.C.A. COMMUNICATIONS, INC.
January 2, 1930.
Admiral C. F. Hughes,
Chief of Naval Operations,
U. S. Navy,
Washington, D. C.
Dear Sir:
Receipt of your letter OP-20-B of December 3lst
regarding interference which we have experienced in
reception of JAA at San Francisco due to both JAA and
Cavite NPo operating on the same frequency, is acknowledged
with thanks for the action you have taken toward inducing
the Japanese to reassign their station.
For your information, I have secured agreement of
the Japanese to change JAA "provisionally" to 19.6 KC
with a promise that if there is no reduction in the
operating effectiveness of the station at that frequency,
it will be made permanent.
Your more formal action in the natter should result
in ensuring the latter and is much appreciated.
Yours very truly,
L. A. BRIGGS,
Traffic Engineer.
C O P Y

R.C.A. COMMUNICATIONS, Ino.
66 Broad Street
New York
January 3, 1930
T-192-430
Captain S. C. Hooper,
Director of Naval Comnunications,
Navy Department,
Washington, D.C.
Dear Captain Hooper:
I wish to refer again to the matter of interference
Tokio, JAA - Cavite, NPO, your reference Op-2O-H of December
30, 1929.
I am glad to be able to advise you that Tokio has
now informed us telegraphically that JAA will remain on 19.6
KC until further notice.
We have been receiving JAA on 19.6
KC at San Francisco during the past ten days and, ad course,
have had no interference from Cavite, NPO.
Thanking you ror the interest and cooperation you
have shown in this matter, I am
Very truly yours,
/s/ L. A. Briggs,
Traffio Engineer.

"The contructin soverme s ahaertake to aid

No. 1
DEPARTIE.T OF FOREIGN AFFAIRS,
Tokyo, January 13, 1930.
Monsieur le Chargé d'Affaires,
I have the honor to acknowledge the receipt of
your Note No. 586 dated December 30, 1929, in which
you cite the instance of a violation by a certain radio
station in Japan of the provisions of Article 17, Section
2, of the General Regulations annexed to the Internation-
al Radio Convention concluded at Washington on November
25, 1927, and request that this matter be brought to the
attention of the appropriate Japanese authorities for
action against the above-mentioned radio station.
In reply I beg to state that in compliance with
your request this matter has promptly been referred to
the appropriate authorities.
I take this opportunity to express to you, Monsieur
le Chargé d'Affaires, the renewed assurance of my high
consideration.
Signed: Baron Kijuro Shidehara
Minister for Foreign Affairs.
Edwin L. Neville, Esquire,
Chargé d'Affaires of the United States
of America.

FAR EASTERN AFFAIRS
MAR 12 1930
MAR 3 30
Sir:
Degartment of State
With further reference to my letter, Op-20-E, A6-2
(10)/mR94(291114) 011830 of January 21st, 1930, the Iwaki Sta-
tion (JAA) of the Japanese Wireless Telegraph Company, Ltd.,
894.74
has been operating on the frequency of 19.6 kcs. for about two
months during which time the Naval Radio Station at Cavite
(NP) has continued working on its frequency of 19.8 kcs.
Considerable interference is being experienced by
the Navy from Station JAA, and it is presumed the Japanese are
likewise getting similar interference from the Naval Radio Sta
tion, NPO.
In accordance with the provisions of the recent Hague
Convention, a tolerance of ± .1%, which with modern equipment
is deemed sufficient to permit the non-interference operation
between the frequencies of 19.6 kcs. and 19.8 kos., is to be ap-
plied as soon as possible.
The Navy Department has decided to make the necessary
changes at its Cavite equipment to enable that station to operate
within the tolerance laid down by the Hague Convention.
In view of the above, it is requested that the necessary
negotiations be entered into with the Japanese Imperial Govern-
ment with a view to having the equipment at the Iwaki Station
modified to operate within the tolerance agreed upon at the Hague
Convention, so that the interference will be eliminated between
the operations of stations JAA and NPO.
MAR 1
Respectfully,
FILEI
LSL
3
rnest Lee Jahncks
The Honorable,
The Seoretary of State,
ing Seoretary the Navy
Washington, D. C.

the Iwaki Station modified to operate within the tolerance
agreed upon at the Hague Conference of the International
Technical Consulting Committee on Radio Communications, so

=== Page 15 === (confidence 0.97)
Maroh 12 1930
0.22
The Honorable
William R. Castle, Jr.,
American Ambassador,
Tokyo.
Sir1
The Department has received a letter dated Maroh 1,
力
1930, from the Aoting Seoretary of the Navy concerning
certain interference caused to Naval Radio Station NPO
力
at Cavite by the Iwaki Station JAA of the Japanese Wire-
less Telegraph Company, Limited. The letter states that
the Iwaki Station has been operating on the frequency of
19.6 kilooyoles for about two months during whioh time
the Station at Cavite has continued working on its frequency
of 19.8 kilooyoles, that considerable interference is being
experienced by the Navy from Station JAA, and that it 1s
presumed that the Japanese are getting similar interference
from the Naval Radio Station, NPO.
The Acting Seoretary of the Navy points out that, in
accordance with the advice of the recent Hague Conference
of the International Teohnical Consulting Committee on Radio
Communications, e tolerance of ± .1%, whioh with modern
equipment is deemed sufficient to permit the non-interference
operation between the frequencies of 19.6 kilooycles and
19.8

=== Page 16 === (confidence 0.97)
-2-
19.s kiloeyoles, is to be applied as soon as possible. He
aleo states that the Navy Department has decided to make
the necessary changes in its Cavite equipment to enable
that station to operate within the tolerance laid down in
the advice of the Hague Conference.
The Department encloses two copies of a translation
of the minutes of the two plenary sessions of the Hague
Conference and refers to page eleven, where a table
including the frequencies mentioned, as well as the
suggestion that the tolerance of ± .1% should be
applied, is found.
In view of the above considerations and in compliance
with the request of the Navy Department, you are instruoted
to bring the matter to the attention of the appropriate
Japanese authorities and to express the hope that the
necessary action will be taken to have the equipment of
the Iwaki Station modified to operate within the tolerance
agreed upon at the Hague Conference, so that interference
will be eliminated between the operations of Stations JAA
and NPO.
I am, Sir,
Your obedient servant,
For the Aoting Secretary of State:

DEPARTMENTOFTHENAYY

WASHINGTON
27 March 1930
April 14. 1930.
Sir:
With further reference to my letter,A6-2(10)/
NR94(291114), of March 1, 1930, and your 1etter,S0 894.
74/77 of March 12, 1930, relative to interference now
existing between the Japanese radio station at Iwaki
(JAA) and the naval radio station at Cavite (NPO),it is
believed that inasmuch as the Japanese Imperial Govern-
ment is a signatory power, the attention of that Govern-
ment should be invited to Article l0 of the Internation-
94.74
al Radiotelegraph Convention of Washington 1927 and to
Article 4, paragraphs 2, 3 ana 4 of the General Regula-
tions annexed thereto.
It is requested that negotiations be entered
into with that Government with a view to suggesting that
C
the Japanese Wireless Telegraph Company, Limited, elimi-
nate the now obsolete compensating wave feature of its
apparatus at Iwaki or, at least, keep it below 19.6
kilocycles in order to prevent interference to Cavite.
As long as the arcs at Iwaki and Cavite are
kept in operation, the Navy Department recognizes the
fact that it will be a difficult matter to maintain a
separation of 200 cycles between them. However, this
separation is believed possible provided the two stations
maintain their frequencies to within the tolerances recom-
mended by the C.C.I.R. In this connection, the Navy
Department is planning to purchase a modern type of trans-
mitter to replace the arc set at Cavite.
Respectfully,
rnest LeeJannes
The Honorable
The Aoting Secretary of the N y.
PR151930
The Secretary of State.

74
I inquired whether the Department's instruction of
77

V
Navy Department, did not take care of the matter. Com-
mander Fisher stated that the important point made in
the letter under discussion was contained in the second
paragraph and that if the request that Iwaki be kept
APR 161030

the request contained in the letter from the Navy De-
partment looks to a temporary arrangement, whereas the
recent instruction to Tokyo looks to a permanent arrange-
ment.
Commander Fisher stated that the Cavite station is
suffering right along from interference and that it is
very

My dear r. Secretary:
Referring to your letter, S0 894.74/84 of July
14, 1930, which cortains enc osures which indicate that
the Iwaki radio station JAA of the Japanese Wireless
Telegraph Company, Ltd. is prepared to maintain its
assigned frequency on 19.6 and within the tolerances
prescribed by The Hacue recommendations.
Since that time interference has been repeated-
ly reported by the Commander-in-Chief Asiatic Fleet and
the Naval authorities operating in the Hawaiian Islands.
A survey of frequency during the month of October indi-
cates that the alternator transmitter of this station is
now perating on a frecuency of 19.75 and the arc is
operaring on a frequency of 19.76 with a compensating
wave on l9.9 kilocycles.
Obviously the two stations, JAA and NPO, are
now so near the same frequency that no equioment can
possibly eparate these two and it is practically im-
possible to effect satisfactory communications on the
United States assigned frequency of l9.8 kilocycles.
It is rumored that the reason the Japanese
station JAA has increased its frequency is because of
the interference which is experienced from the Malabar,
Java, station PLA, which appears to be a reasonable
assumption.
It is again requested that proper reoresenta-
tion be made to obtain a satisfactory freguency separation
between these two stations.
Sincerely yours,
Hanlttrls
Honorable Henry L. Stimson,
Secretary of State.

January 5, 1931
8
Reply-:1/14/31
Division of
FAR EASTERN AFFAIRS
re-mere
The Secretary of State
Washington, D. C.
JA161931
Dear Sir:
8
We are regretfully obliged to protest to the
Department of State certain violations of the Interna-
tional Radiotelegraph Convention by the Japanese govern-
ment, and to seek the aid of the Department in obtaining
relief.
The International Radiotelegraph Convention,
signed at Washington in 1927, assigns the frequency bands
from 7,000 ko. to 7,300 kc., and from 14,000 kc. to
14,400 ko. exclusively to amateur stations. The Japanese
government has installed a considerable number of stations
in the first-mentioned band, and at least one in the second-
mentioned band, in violation of the convention, and with
the result that considerable interference to amateur cor-
respondence is resulting, particularly in our Western
states.
The Berne List shows the registration by Japan
of Station JBK at Kagoshima on 7040 ko., amongst other fre-
quencies. It similarly shows station JEw at Osaka register-
ed for 7160 ko. and 14,320 ko. amongst others; station JFB
at Taiholku for 7180 kc. amongst others; and station JYT at
Tokyo for 7220 ko. amongst others. In addition to these
stations, stations JEs at Osaka and JFA at Taihoku are op-
orating in the 7,000-7,300 ko. band.
7.5
JAN 2 1 1931
These stations seem to use considerable power.
FILED
They do not all operate on the registered frequencies but
do operate somewhere within the amateur band, apparently
occasionally changing frequency. Their signals for the
most part are broad and of a highly interfering nature.
B
is a serious inconvenience to amateur correspondence, par-
tioularly in our Western States, and many members of this
society have appealed to this office for relief.

=== Page 54 === (confidence 0.99)
Seoretary of State,
- 2 -
January 5, 1931
The suspicion exists in our minds that the
Japanese government misinterprets the convention and
considers that the bands in question, while authorized
for amateur use, are available for other purposes if an
administration does not elect to assign them to amateurs.
This of course is not true.
None of the above-listed stations is an amateur
station. They seem to be either government or commercial
stations, sometimes possibly with a dual status.
We respectfully address ourselves to the Depart-
ment in an endeavor to secure relief from this violation
of the convent ion of Washington, and request that the De-
partment endeavor to have the Japanese government remove
all non-amateur stations from these exclusively amateur
bands. We shall be highly appreciative.
Respectfully yours,
The AMERICAN RADIO RELAY LEAGUE, Inc.
By
KBW:JHK
Secretary

894.74/9
Dear Sir:
894.
Please be referred to your file TD: 984.
74/88. On January 5, 193l, we wrote to you protesting
certain violations of the International Radiotelegraph
Convention by the Japanese government, in the operation
of stations within bands internationally assigned ex-
clusively to amateur stations, and asking the aid of the
Department in obtaining relief from this interference.
Cn January 20, l931, the Department acknowledged, under
the above file, asking for more complete and specific
dat to establish definite interference.
Heroinafter in this letter I transmit to you
information on speoific cases of interference to amateur
operation caused by the operation of the Japanese stations
in question, showing the exact time, place and nature of
the interference, and the amateur correspondence interfer-
ed with. There are, it will be seen, a large number of
Japanese non-amateur stations operating in the exclusively
amateur band from 7,000 kc. to 7,300 kc. In addition to
FLED
the stations herein reported, we have received scattering
complaints from our members on certain other Japanese sta-
tions, but without insufficient data so far to back them.
Further in addition to the stations herein reported, it is
noted in the Berne Frequency List: that the Japanese govern-
ment has registered Station JEw at Osaka on the frequencies
of 7160 kc. and 14,320 kc.; and station JYT at Tokyo on the
frequenoy of 722o kc. These stations are not yet reported
as interfering in this country but have been nétified to
Berne as operating on frequencies which are similarly with-
in the amateur bands.
These Japanese stations cause a serious and de-
structive interference with amateur work throughout the
territory west of the Rooky Mountains, inoluding United
States territories and possessions in the Pacific. Most

=== Page 68 === (confidence 0.99)
The Secretary of State,
- 2 -
May 7, 1931
of the signals are very broad, indicating frequenoy modulation
and the employment of outmoded methods. The transmitters, for
the most part, are unstable and frequently change operating
frequenoy. It will be noted that station JrH causes the most
serious interference to amateur operation, operating with high
power approximately in the center of this international amateur
band and causing many interruptions and much embarrassment to
amateur communications. All of these are Japanese stations:
their exact location in Japan is unknown.
Whereas the Japanese government may be entitled to
operate stations in the small bands internationally reserved
exclusively for amateurs provided no interference results with
other stations, it is now demonstrated that interference of the
most serious nature does exist, and that the Japanese govern-
ment thereby is violating the International Radiotelegraph Con-
vention of Washington to which it is a party. Wherefore we
again respectfully request the Department of State to protest
the operation of Japanese non-amateur stations in the bands
internationally assigned exclusively to amateurs, and to pro-
vide relief to us by causing the operation of these stations
in these amateur bands to be discontinued.
Specific data follow, arranged alphabetically by
interfering stations.

The Department is informed by the Secretary of
the American Radio Relay League that the operation of
certain Japanese radio stations has been causing serious
interference with the communications of amateur stations
in this country situated west of the Rooky Mountains.
An annex is appended hereto containing specific data
which has been obtained by the American Radio Relay
League concerning instances of interference and the
nature thereof, as well as conoerning the stations re-
ported to be causing the interference.
The American Radio Relay League also points out
that in addition to the stations which have thus far
been oausing interference, there are certain other
Japanese stations, including Station JEW at Osaka and
Station JYT at Tokyo, which are indicated in the Berne
frequenoy lists as registered on frequencies within the
amateur bands.
A general

=== Page 75 === (confidence 0.95)
-2
A gemeral eharacteristie of the interfering
statioms appears to be that their signals have a
vesy broad note, indicating frequeney modulation.

=== Page 77 === (confidence 0.99)
MANILA DAILY BULLETIN
February 18, 1931.
or of Posts
Aa, P. I.
Dear Sir:
I have just recently obtained a license to operate an Amateur station here
in Manila. Upon getting my station on the air I find the amateur frequencies
already occupied by Commercial stations. In fact there is such an aggregation
of commercial stations in the band thet it is extremely difficult to operate
therein. The 7000 to 7300 kc band is the one of which I speak. Any day or
night...and this is not an exaggeration...one can tune in nd find commercial
stations within the limits of the amateur band.
Such stations as JES : JCK : JBK : JFN : JFH, and others may be found
operating with impunity inside the amateur band. Sometimes it is possible to
hear KAM onerating in this band also. Then I might go into an endless ennumera-
tion of "F", and "R", and "X" prefix commercials whom I have heard on amateur
territory. If it is necessary to list all of the offending commercials and
the countries to which they belong in order to get them removed from our bands,
the amateur is a declining class, for such a tabulation would be impossible.
It would seem to mark the end of the Amateur if we are to permit these commer-
cial stations to operate on our frequencies without challenge.
The amateurs were "fleeced" at te last International Radiotelegraphic
Convention merely because the Commercial interests proved to the satisfaction
of the "powers that be", that the Amateur could get along on less room. Simi-
larly, at the next Convention, another slice of the "so called" amateur fre-
quencies will be added to commercial territory to be used for the extremely
elevating pastime of sending "V" and dots, by the hour. Of course the world
could not possibly got along without these indispensable "dot" stations, but
the Amateurs, who are not contriuting such vast guantities of scientific
information to the "Radio World" can be eliminated.
In the States, from which I have recently come, and where I held both
Amateur, and Commercial licenses, I was qui te active in "Air Policing" work
and I am proud to say that VERY few amateurs, in proportion to the total
number, were guility of operatins on commercial bends. In f.ct any wilful
lawlossness of this nature is punishable by loss of license, nd possiuly
fines and imprisonment. How about the law working both ways?
Would it be possible to get some action in this matter before it is
too late?
It had not occurred to me before, but I wonder if the distance of the
Orient from the Radio Centers of the world has anyth ng to do with lax
enforcement of Radio Laws. Or am I wrong in assuming there to be any law
at all that applies to these "Air Pirates" of the Orient?
Sincerely hoping for some immediate change for the better, I am
Yours truly,
I. S. Liher
W6EIZ - W6EKE
KA 1 SL

Honsieur,
Par lettre du 30 janvier ecoule vous nous demandez
d'intervenir, si faire se peut, aupres des instances
competentes, afin qu'elles prennent les mesures necessai-
res pour assurer aux amateurs le libre usage des ondes
qui leur sont reservees par le Reglement general annexe
a la Convention radiotelegraphique de Washington, c'est-a-
dire pour remedier aux brouillages causes par des sta-
tions radioelectriques commerciales.
En reponse a votre demande, nous avons l'honneur de
vous faire savoir que notre Bureau n'a pas qualite pour
entreprendre les demarches sollicitees; le controle des
stations radioelectriques est du ressort exclusif de
l'administration du pays dans lequel se trouvent ces
stations. Nous vous engageons a adresser vos doleances
aux autorites dont dependent les stations radioelectriques
des Iles Philippines afin que ces autorites puissent faire
les representations utiles aupres de l'Administration
japonaise, conformement a l'article 12 du Reglement
general.
Veuillez agreer, Monsieur, l'assurance de notre con-
sideration distinguee.

LIANILA DAILY BULLETIN
January 30, 1931.
International Radiotelegraphic Bureau
Geneva, Switzerland.
Gentlemen:
Enclosed find a copy of a letter which I have sent
to the Minister of Communications of Japan in care of the
Japanese Consul General here in Hanila. The letter is
self explanatory.
Would it be possible for you to give the Amateurs
any assistance in this matter?
You are no doubt aware of the fact that the present
Amateur bands are insufficiently wide to permit "comfort-
able" conversation to Amateurs alone, much less to us
who have to compete with the Commercial "V" or "dot" ma-
chines or traffic stations.
If we Amateurs were to continue to permit Commercial
stations to trespass upon our property it would be but a
short time before we would be forced to work within a
still smaller "slice of the spectrum" which would practi-
cally mean extinction.
Would it not be the same principle if Amateurs were
to monopolize the commercial frequencies by sending
"hash" in these forbidden bands as they are now doing in
ours? Yet, if an Amateur were to attempt such a course
of action, or even make a mistake and unwittingly operate
outside the so-called "Amateur" bands, his station would
be closed immediately and his license revoked.
Would it be overstepping the bounds of propriety
to request that our "Amateur" bands be not only AMATEUR
in title but in reality?
Thanking you in advance for some positive action
and a bit of encouragement, I am
Sincerely,
(SGD.) I.S. LINER
W6EKE , W6EIZ,

MANILA DAILY BULLET IN
January 30, 1931.
Minister of Communications
Japan,
Care of Japanese Consul-General
Manila, P.I.
Dear Sir:-
I should like to inquire as to what right stations
JBK, JES, JFH, and others, operate within the limits of
the Amateur 700o to 730o kilocycle band.
I have recently come to lanila from Los Angeles,
California where I was quite active in "Official Observer"
and other "Air policing" work. During the several years
which I spent on this work, I enabled many hundreds of
amateurs to calibrate wavemeters and other frequency
measuring devices so as to be better able to keep within
the limits of our non-too-large slice of the spectrum.
Several times I have had occasion to check the
frequencies of commercial stations which were operating
within the limits of our bands, and, upon notifying them
of their encroachment upon Amateur property, they would
immediately check up on the offending wavelengths and
return to their own proper channels.
For the past month of my stay here in Ilanila, I
have observed the Amateur band to be crowded with Japanese
Commercial stations. Some send "V's," some "dots," others
merely send "QRk?" by the hour. I believe that I am
correct in assuming Japan to be a member of the Interna-
tional Radiotelegraphic Convention, since she has signed
the agreements drawn up by that body. Therefore, it is
at once apparent that JBK, JES, and the others are erro-
neously trying to hold frequencies which, by the terms of
the agreement, are already assigned to the Amateurs of
the world.
Would it be in order to request that a check be made
upon this situation immediately, and steps taken to eli-
minate the interference? On behalf of the Amateurs of
the Philippine Islands, I wish to state than I am sure
no encroachment will be made by the Amateurs upon your

=== Page 86 === (confidence 0.99)
-2-
commercial frequencies if you will remove the com-
mercial stations from our Amateur bands and thereby give
us room in which to operate.
Looking forward to prompt action in this matter,
I am
Sincerely,
(SGD.) I.S. LINER
W6EKEW6EIZ

EMBASSY OF THE UNITED STATES OF AMERICA
No. 90
Tokyo, June 16, 1931.
Excellenoy:
I have the honor, under instructions from my
Government, to inform Your Excellency that the Depart-
ment of State has been informed by the Secretary of the
American Radio Relay League that the operation of cer-
tain Japanese radio stations has been causing serious
interference with the communications of amateur stations
in the United States situated west of the Rocky Mountains.
There is enclosed for the information of the authorities
concerned an annex containing specifio data which has
been obtained by the American Radio Relay League con-
cerning instances of interference and the nature thereof,
as well as concerning the stations reported to be causing
the interference.
The American Radio League points out that in
addition to the stations whioh have thus far been causing
interference, there are certain other Japanese stations,
including Station JEW at Osaka and Station JYT at Tokyo,
which are indicated in the Berne frequenoy lists as
registered on frequencies within the amateur bands.

appears to be that their signals have a very broad note,

reported as unstable for the most part, resulting in
frequent changes in operating frequencies.

Sir:
The Department has received a letter dated Decem-
ber 22, 1931, from the Secretary of War, stating that
the Japanese Govermment radio station at Tokyo (JIT) has
recently initiated operation on the frequenoy of 8l60
kilooycles. This frequenoy has been in use by the War
Department radio station WTJ at Fort Shafter, Hawaii,
since June, 1928, and the recepsion of this etation is
being interfered with by station JYT. The list of
frequensies published by the Iatermational Burean of
the Telegraph Union in April, 1930, shows that the
frequemey 8l60 kilooyoles was notified to that Bureem
for statien wTd on June 28, 1928.
Beth Japan and the United States are parties to
the International Radio Gonvention signed at Washington
in 1027. Artiele 10, paragraph 2 of that convestion
provides as follows:
«all statiens, whatever their purpess,
must, se far as prastieable, be established
Artiale 8

=== Page 108 === (confidence 0.94)
Artiele 5, paragraph 16 (1) of the General Regula-
tiens annexed to the Gonvention provides:
"The frequeneies assigned by Administrations
to all new fixed land or radio breadoasting stations
which they may have authorised or of which they may
have undertaken the installation must be ohosen in
such a manner as to prevent so far as practicable
interference with intornational services carried
on by existing stations the frequeneies of which
have already been notified to the International
Burean. In the case of a change of the frequenoy
of an existing fixed land or broadeasting station,
the new frequenoy assigned to this station must
comply with the above conditions."
Please call the above faots to the attention of
the Japanese Government in order that the interference
with the operation of station wTJ may be eliminated.
Very truly yours,
For the Seoretary of State:

NEW RADIO BROADCASTING ST.TI N AT KOKURA, JAPA:
From Henry B. Hitchcock, Consul.
Nagasaki, Japan.
Date of Completion: December 29, L931
Date of Mailing:
December 31, L931.
On December 2l, l93l, a new radio broadcasting station was
32
opened at Kokura, Japan. Kokura is but a few iles south of Moji on
the Moji-Kagoshimm railway line and is the center of a thickly
populated industrial area. It is expected that the new station will
enable a much larger number of persons that hitherto to enjoy radio
reception with sets of moderate prices.
When the first radio broadcasting station in Kyushu was opened
in Kumamoto in l928, it was believed that it would serve the whole
island. The physiography of the island is such, however, that re-
coption was possible only to very sensitive sets, excopt in especially
favored localities. The plan now is to open a number of small broad-
casting stations at suitable intervals about the island in places
where they will compensate for the poor recption from the Kumamoto
station. Those stations will for the most part content themselves with

=== Page 111 === (confidence 0.98)
-2-
rebroadcasting the Kumamoto programs except whon there are occasionally
items of local interest available. Such a station was the one opened
in Fukuoka in l930, as is also the one just opened in Kokura. Others
are to be opened later in Nagasaki and Ka oshima.
The new statior. at Kokura is uuilt on a high nill where t will
have the maximum of freedor from interference. The structures cost
bout 120,000 yen ($60,000). The main buildin s  reinforced
concrete of fire-proof ane earthquake-proef constructien. fhe tri-
angular, skeleton masts are ) meters high anu are set 86 meters
apart. The broadcasting equipment was aesigned and ade n Tokyo by
the Tokyo Denki Kaisha (Tokyo Electric Company) and is tne rirst set
of this type to pe designed and manufactured by the coupany. iven the
parts are said to pe entirely of Japanese manuiacture. the voltage of
6000 is controlled simply by push-buttons. fhere is an automtic cir-
cuit-breaker which operates in case of trouole. Further details are
as followss
Name of station
JOSK
Wave length
408 meters
Kilocycles
735
Power
1000 watts
A crystal broadcasting set is used in order to obviate so far as
possible interference from other stations in Kyushu.
The area within which crystal receiving sets may be used now
includes the most populus part of Kyushu. While there are few persons
a
of great wealth, there are relatively large number of persons of moderate
1
means who couid well afford the choap sets necessary to receive from the
Kokura station.
The new station will stimulate for a time the sales of cheap radio
sets. This may to some extent promote the sale of American-made radio

=== Page 112 === (confidence 0.97)
-3-
parts and accessories out hardly of complete sets.
The high import
duty, the unfavorable rate of exchange, and the low purchasing power
of the people, as compared with the Americar. arket, would tend to
operate against this.

January 18, 1932.
FE
The instructions have been addressed to the Consulates
in the Dutch East Indies and French Indo-China rather than
to The Hague and to Paris because the Navy seeks merely to
have the radio stations involved check their operating
freguencies so that they may be aware of the extent of
their deviation from the assigned frequencies. A suggestion
00
from the Consul to the local authorities or even to the
managers of the stations involved will be sufficient to
accomplish this. There is no suggestion on the part of
the Navy that the foreign stations are trying to preempt
frequencies assigned to American radio stations. Presumably
the stations will take steps to prevent the deviation when
they become aware of the fact of the deviation.
JAN 26 1932

a Japanese station and your reply thereto dated January 5, 1932,

894.74/101
Under date of February 1l, 1932, the Commanding General
of the Philippine Department advised that the frequency of 8160
kcs is being utilized by Japanese stations JYR, JYV and JYT on
such high power as to often entirely disrupt communication from
Honolulu to Manila. He has further advised that these Japanese
stations appear to handle no traffic whatever, simply sending Vs
and umanswered calls.

caused by non-essential emissions, it is recommended that more
urgent representations be made to the Japanese Administration to
effect a change in its frequency assignments.

894.74/108

Naval radio stations of the United States, particularly
on the frequenoy 13308 kilocycles.

here mentioned is a continuation, undiminiened and, if
anything, worse than ever, that has been complained
about on previous occasions. He state: that the use of
naval radio in the Far Eastern territory has been seriously
impaired and that previous attempts to secure the co-

My dear Mr. Secretary:
S
Departmont of State
Naval authorities in the Sixteenth Naval District
report that station "JAQ" has been causing serious in-
21/10/33
terference on 8090 kilooycles daily since 9 January,
beginning about l700 Zone minus eight time, and continu-
ing until about O300, at which time communications in the
Sixteenth Naval District are shifted to another circuit.
The communications are high speed commercial traffic and
the interference is charaoterized as "heavy to obliterative".
When not transmitting traffio, the transmissions are continued
on a "dotter".
As regards the practice of keeping a "dotter" active
during times when not engaged in actual communications, the
opinion is expressed by this Office that this is contrary
to the provisions of Article 20, "Interference", of the Gen-
eral Radiocommunications Regulations, Madrid, 1932.
The Berne lists at hand do not give any information
with respect to the location of this station or the frequen-
ey which is assigned to it, which is taken as an indication
that it is a new tranamitter recently placed in operation.
It is requested that appropriate representation be made to
the administration concerned, with a view to the elimination
of this interference.
Sincerely yours,
FEB
C
C.
Honorable Henry L. Stimson
Secretary of State

Dear Mr. Secretary:
DEPARTMENT BE STATE
Serious daily interference to Trans-Pacific radio channels
894.74/112
of the War Department is being experienced from Japanese radio stations.
Specifically, the frequencies of 806o kc used at San Francisco and
Manila and l6l20 kc used at Manila are being interfered with by trans-
missions on the same frequencies by Station JAQ, location unknown;
also, 8600 and 17200 kc used at San Francisco have been interfered
with by transmissions on the same frequencies by Station JFI, Taihoku.
The International Priority of the United States, in
registration and use of all the frequencies in question, is estab-
lished in the International rrequency List and it is requested that
diplomatic representations be made to the Japanese Administration
with a view to the elimination of this interference.
The International Frequency List indicates that Japanese
radio stations have utilized some of the frequencies in question
for a considerable time, whereas, the interference experienced
is relatively recent. These facts indicate that the interference
results from a recent increase in power or change in operating
conditions. It may be advisable to bring this to the attention of
the Japanese Administration as a return to the status existing
prior to the time interference developed may be more easily
FEB
accomplished than a complete change in frequency assignment.

=== Page 152 === (confidence 0.98)
Enclosure No
to despatch
No287 of7i.,..33
from the
Tmbasev at Tokyo.
CO P Y
MEMORANDUM.
Reference is made to the letter of Mr. Neville,
dated December 14, 1932, to the Vice Minister for
Foreign Affairs, and to Mr. Arita's reply of January 13,
1933, regarding the interference which the United States
Naval radio stations in the Philippines are experiencing
on the frequency band of 13305 kilocycles from stations
JES and JEA at Osaka. It appears that the American
Naval stations are operating within the band permitted
them by international agreement, while the Osaka stations,
which are allotted the frequency band of 13280 kilo-
cycles by the Berne Bureau, are operating outside of their
assigned frequency band, thus conflicting with the American
Naval stations. In Mr. Arita's reply of January 13, 1933,
he stated that the appropriate authorities had informed
him that the interference had been caused by an ineffective
control system in the Osaka stations, but that the defect
had been remedied and that it was believed that no more
interference would be experienced.
The Embassy has today received a telegram from
the Department of State, stating that the interference
from Japanese radio stations, particularly on the fre-
quency of 13308 kilocycles, has increased rather than
diminished recently and that the interference is now so
serious as greatly to impair the operating efficiency
of the American stations on their assigned frequencies.
It is therefore urgently requested that the Foreign
Office request the competent authorities in the Japanese
Government to take appropriate measures which as soon as
possible will correct the failure of Japanese stations to
remain within the limits of their assigned frequency bands.
Tokyo, January 24, 1933

=== Page 153 === (confidence 0.98)
Enclosure No 久
to despatch
No 287 of 7er.,0, 33 from the
CQPY
Embassy at Tokyo:
Translation.
No. 5/T2
MEMORANDUM.
January 26, 1933.
The Department of Foreign Affairs has the honor
to acknowledge the receipt of a memorandum dated January
24 from the American Embassy in Tokyo stating that ac-
cording to a telegram from the Department of State,
interference between the United States Naval Wireless
Station in the Philippines and the Osaka Wireless Station
has increased rather than decreased and requesting that
the appropriate Japanese authorities take steps to prevent
such interference.
The Department of Foreign Affairs at once transmitted
the sense of this memorandum to the appropriate autho-
rities reguesting the latter to make an investigation. The
Department of Foreign Affairs is now in receipt of a com-
munication from the appropriate authorities, stating that
the Osaka Wireless Station on and after January 15 com-
menced to use sending apparatus of the crystal control
system which was installed during the latter part of last
year. Tests conducted by the Tokyo Wireless Station as to the
frequency used by the Osaka Wireless Station show that 13,273
Kilocycles were recorded on January 16; 13,279 kilocycles
on January 18; 13,280 kilocycles on January 19; and 13,280
kilocycles on January 20 and 21. From this it is seen
that since January 15th the frequency used by the Osaka
Wireless Station has been made constant, showing but a
trifling deviation. Accordingly the appropriate authori-
ties hold that no interference occurs at present between
the Osaka Wireless Station and the United States Naval
Wireless Station in the Philippines.

Tokyo, February 20, 1933.
No.84.
Excellency:
I have the honor to inform Your Excellency that my
Government has instructed me to bring to your attention
a case of interference by a Japanese wireless station with
the United States Neval wir less stations.
The United States Naval authoritie: in the Philippine
Islands report that they are xperiencing serious inter-
ference from station JAt in Japan on the frequency of 80-90
kilocycles. This interference has been experienced daily
since January 9, 1933, beginning about 6 a.m., Janan time,
and continuing until about 4 a.m., at which time wireless
communications between the Naval stations is shifteu to another
circuit. The communications carried by the interfering
station are high speed commercial traffic, nd when the
station is not transmitting traffic, the transmissions are
continued on a "dotter". The interference is stated to be
"heavy to obliterative". The frequency lists do not show
such a station as "JAQ" and no frequency is assigned to this
station , which makes it appear that this is a new station
not yet listed or assigned a frecuency band.
I request Your Excellency to be kind enough to bring
this matter to the attention of the appronriate authorities
with a view to eliminating the interference of which com-
plaint is made.
I avail myself of this opportunity to renew to Your
Excellency the assurances of my highest consideration.

The Secretary of State.
Dear Mr. Secretary:
DEPARTMENT OF STATE
The Secretary of War has requested me to aclnow-
ledge the receipt of your letter of April 26, 1933, file TD:
894.74/119. It is believed that the discontinuance of the use
894.74/120
by Japanese stations of 8060 kc and 8600 kc will greatly alleviate
interference conditions in the Pacific area. 1t is possible that
interference on 16,120 kc, which is the second harmonic of 8060 kc,
will disappear when the latter frequency is changed.
With reference to the informal suggestion of the
Japanese Government that interference matters be discussed by
experts of the American and Japanese Governments, it is believed
that matters concerning interference between stations assigned
identical frequencies should be confined to diplomatic channels
as no technical considerations are involved in interferences re-
sulting from duplicate assignments in the same area.
In this connection, reference is made to a communi-
cation from the Assistant Secretary of State dated March 5, 1932,
reference TD, and in which it was stated that the Japanese Depart-
ment of Communications would communicate directly with the interest-
ed parties with reference to the objectional interference. To date,
so far as can be ascertained, no action has been taken by the De-
partment of Communications.
Sincerely yours,
D0S-44.

ADDRESS REPLY TO

WASHINGTON LLAIY UUN
20 Wa. 7 FRC 64 33
AFFADS
of achro.
3 June 1933
519
duw
9
My dear Mr. Secretary
JUN 533
DEPARTMENT OF STATE
5
Reference is made to State Department letter of 26 April file
TD:894:74/119, tran smitting copy of dis patch No. 345, dated 30 March
Trom the Ambassador at Tokyo, together with its enclosures, and re-
questing the comment of this department upon the informal suggestion
made by the Japanese Foreign Office that it might be advisable to have
a discussion between experts of the two countries on the subject of
radio interference.
8
6
This department believes that such a discussion, if suitably limi-4
ted in scope, may be of va lue in helping to eliminate the serious radio.
interference now experienced in the Far East, and, subject to satisfac-
tory arrangements in detail, welcomes the suggestion thus made.
4
It appears that th3 problem to be dealt with is solely that of the—
interference which Uniiad States radio stations in the Orient, Hawaii, N
and the West Coast of the United States are experienc ing from the operat
tion of Japanese stations. It therefore appears desirable to limit the
scope of discussion to questions of such interference, including the
question of the utilization of radio frequency channels, but excluding
all broader aspects of the general subject of radio communications and
excluding also the question of general frequency allocations. Such
limitation of the agenda should obviate the necessity of sending rep-
resentatives from Washington, with attendant expense and delay, and by
avoiding other controversial questions should tend to promote the early
success of the proposed conference.
So far as this department is aware, the only United States radio
stations concerned are those of the Army and Navy, and within this area
the Navy has by far the larger number of stations and volume of radio
traffic. This department will be able to designate a suitably qualified
officer among those on duty on the Asiatic station, and it is understood
that the Army can make available from Manila an officer similarly quali-
fied. It is, therefore, suggested that the United States representation
at this conference consist only of officers who may at the time be on
duty in the Far East in the service of the interested departments, and
in view of the Navy's large interest in the question at issue, it is
further suggested that the senior American representative be chosen from
the Naval Service.
LEM

=== Page 183 === (confidence 0.94)
一 2 -
So far as this department is concerned, it will be most conven-
Tont if arrangements can be made to hold the discussion with the
Japanese representatives at Shanghai during the latter part of August.
In case your department concurs as to the desirability of the
proposed conference, early notification of the intended action will

2

5
A reply of June 3, 1933, from the Secretary of the
Navy, indicates that the Navy Department approves the

FLED

1933
The pertinent papers on the subject are attached
hereto.
The opinion of the Division of Far Eastern Affairs
in connection with this matter is desired. In the event
that no objection is perceived to the proposals, it is

=== Page 192 === (confidence 0.96)
-2-
suggested that the Division of Far Eastern Affairs may
wish to handle the matter, with a view to facilitating
arrangements for a meeting of the nature in question.
The attached letters could then go forward if you approve.
It may be noted that at present Navy tries through
the State Department to get Japanese stations to shift
their frequencies. If no results are obtained in a
reasonable time, it is understood that Navy proceeds to
"burn" the Japanese station off the Navy frequency,
i.e., to interfere incessantly with the Japanese station
until the latter moves its operations. A further con-
sideration is that some of the Japanese stations may
possibly be military and naval stations, and the parties
to the radio convention reserved their liberty of action
with respect to such stations. Up to the present, the
Japanese have never relied upon this reserved liberty of
action. If they do so at a future time, there may result
a "burning" contest between particular Japar&se and Ameri-
can military stations to see which can drive the other off
the desired frequency.

WESTERN EDRUPEAN AFFAIRS
PC:
S
You will note from the attached correspondence that
the War and Navy Departments have been experiencing con-
894.74/1
siderable difficulty in the transmission of radio mes-
sages in the Far East on account of interference from
certain Japanese radio stations.
On February 20, 1933, and at other times the Ameri-
26
can Embassy at Tokyo attempted to settle the difficulty
through diplomatic channels, but apparently was unsuccess-
ful. In discussing the question with the Japanese Foreign
Office, however, an officer of the Embassy was informed
that the Japanese felt that it might be advisable to have
the matter discussed at a conference attended by experts
of Japan and the United States. The War and Navy Depart-
ments have agreed that such a conference is desirable
FILED
(see attached letter from Navy Department under date
311933
June 3, and letter from War Department under date
June 14, 1933). It will be noted, however, that items
such as expenses of delegates et cetera, are not touched upon
in

=== Page 196 === (confidence 0.96)
-2-
in the letters under reference.
As the case now appears to have reached the stage
where it falls within the scope of international
conferences, FE feels that the matter should be turned
over to PC for future action. FE is inclined to
believe that a logical method of dealing with the
Commiealewith
matter would be to wite lottore to the War and Navy
Departments suggesting that the conference be informal
and that after mutual consideration and discussion they
inform this Department of their opinion as to a suitable
place and date for the conference, number of American
representatives, agenda and any other facts which
appear to be pertinent. The War and Navy Departments
might also be requested to make definite commitments
to the effect that they will send and pay the expenses
of the delegates from their Departments. When the above
information and commitments have been received, FE suggests
that the Department instruct the American Embassy at Tokyo
to approach informally the Japanese Foreign Office on the
subject and endeavor to work out suitable arrangements.
FE is particularly interested in the case on account
of the present complicated situation in the Far East. It
will be appreciated, therefore, if you will keep this
Division currently informed of such action as you may take.

Tokyo, June 8, 1933.
No. 124.
Excellency:
I have the honor to refer to previous corres-
pondence on the subject of wireless telegraph inter-
ferences and to inform Your Exccllency that my Gov-
ernment has instructed me to invite attention to two
more csses of serious interference by Japanese wire-
less stations.
The first of these interfe ences occurred on May
14, when the Japanese Radio station JCS(at Choshi),
which according to information received by the Embassy
was assigned frequency 8200 by the Berne Bureau, was
observed by the United States Naval station to be oper-
ating on frequency 8lo0. The second c se of inter-
ference occurred on May 21, when the Japanese sta-
tion JAQ (in Tokyc), which it is understood has been
assigned frequency o60, was observed to be operating
for about three hours on frcquency 8089. As Your Ex-
cellency cun readily appreci te such interference pre-
vented efficient communicstion on frequency 8090 which
has been assigned to thc United States Navy.
I therefore have the honor to request Yeur Excel-
lency to be kind enough to bring this matter once more
to the attention of the appropriate authorities in or-
der that the interferences of which complaints have
been made may be eliminated.
I avail myself of this opportunity to renew to
Your Excellency the assurances of my highest considera-
tion.
Signed: Joseph C. Grew

September 18, 1933.
Lieut. Commander Stone called me up on
the phone on September 1st, o ask whether
any reply had been received from Tokyo to
the telegram proposing that a conference be
held on october 1 (Department's 70,July 29).
I said that none had been received.' e
then said that his Department was in no hurry
about the Conference, and in renly to my
suggestion of send ing a telegraphic inquiry,
he replied that his Department would prefer
not to hurry the matter.
It is to be noted that the War Department,
which is still having trouble with radio
intereference, concurs in the  avy Denartments
recommendation to defer the Jonference.
No immediate action appears to be
Stab
necessary.

=== Page 220 === (confidence 1.00)
DEPARTMENT OF STATE
DIVISION OF FAR EASTERN AFFAIRS
It occurred to me while in Tokyo that
it was possible that the persistent inter-
ference with our Far Eastern Naval radio
communications by Japanese stations (some
of which appeared to be new and unknown)
might be intentional. In time of war the
Japanese would undoubtedly make every
effort to cut the radio and cable com-
munications of our Asiatic Fleet with the
home stations; it is possible that the
Japanese stations of which complaint is
made were experimenting to ascertain the
effectiveness of their interference.
Having ascertained from our complaints
that their interference is entirely ef-
feotive, they have stopped the inter-
ference.
This is only a guess, unsupported by
any facts except that the interference did
take place when relations between Japan
and the Western nations were somewhat
strained.
ERD

My dear Mr. Secretary:
894.74/130
Please refer toAmyrettentApr 12 July, 1933, concern-
ing the proposed radio conference with the Japanese, at Tokyo,
in October or later.
894.74/135
The Navy Department desires to inform the State
Department that the Navy has recently experienced very little
radio interference from Japanese stations. The Army cannot
report a similar clearing up of radio interference from Japan-
ese stations, but nevertheless it appears desirable to the
Navy Department to wait until about 1 December, 1933 before
deciding whether or not a conference with the Japanese is
necessary. The Navy will continue to prepare for a confer-
ence should one with the Japanese be arranged.
The War Department has indicated concurrence with
the Navy Department's recommendation to defer decision concern-
ing the holding of a conference until about 1 December, 1933.
Sincerely yours,

The Japanese authorities stated that they do not
consider it necessary to hold an informal conference of
experts to discuss questions of radio interference, They
suggest that future complaints of interference be sent
direct to this Embassy by the American Army and Navy
authorities in the Orient, to be transmitted to the Denart-
ment of Communications who will make an immediate investi-
gation and report to this Embassy. The Naval Attache who
has by my instructions conferred with the Department of
Communications believes that this suggestion offers the
best method of settling future cases of interference.

=== Page 230 === (confidence 0.99)

DIVISION OF FAR EASTERN AFFAIRS
September 27, 1933.
I read to Major Colton of the War De-
partment Tokyo's telegram No. l47, Septem-
ber 27, 10 a.m., with regard to "wireless
interference conference". Major Colton
said that he had serious objections to the
plan outlined in the telegram of authorizing
the local military authorities in the
Philippines to communicate directly with the
Embassy. He said that, although the Navy
had reported an improvement in interference
conditions, the Army stations were having
more trouble than ever before. It was his
feeling that if no conference is to be held
that we should revert to the old practice
of transmitting complaints through the usual
official channels. He added that several
Army stations in the United States were
complaining of interference and it would be
obviously difficult to authorize their com-
manding officers to communicate with Tokyo
without previous reference to the War De-
partment.
Major Colton said that he would com-
municate with the Department after he had
received a copy of the telegram under reference,
a copy of which is being forwarded to the
War Department.

Dear Mr. Secretary:
Reference is m de to letter from tne Chief of Staff to the
Secretary of State dated May 10, 19s3, to letter from the Secretary
894.74/137
of War to the Secretary oi State dated Jun 14, 1933, concerning
Japanese radio interrerence, and to subseguent correspondence on this
subject, particularly to dispatcn No. 147 dated September 27, 1933,
from the American Anbassador at Tokyo to the Secretary or State, and
to letter from the Secretary of Navy to the Secretary of State file
Op-20-E (9-9-33) in which it was indicated that both the War Depart-
ment and the Navy Department desired to derer decision concernin the
holding of a conference until about December 1, 1933.
The suyestion or the Japanese authorities, contained in the
dispatch from the merican Ambassador, No. 147, that future complaints
of interference be reportea direct to the nbassy by the merican Mili-
tary and Naval autnorities in t.e Crient is not concurred in. The
interference in question affects not only Army ana Navy radio stations
in the Philippine Department but also Aray radio stations in the Hawaiian
Department, in the Ninth Corps Area, those under t c conurol o1 the
Officer in Charge or the Waslin ton-Alaska Military Cab e and Telegraph
System, and certain laval stations outside of the Orient.
It is not believed tnat tne lilitary and Naval authorities in
the Orient are in position to coordinate correspondence concerning
inter erence resulting at stations not under tneir command and it is
considered doubtful that tnese authorities would have at all times
adequate information upon wnich to base deci ions, even though they were
in position to originate or cooruinate routine reports concerning inter-
ference.
It is believed te course of events since tne initiation of
steps looking toward a conierence on tne radio interference involved
has amply demonstrated that it is impractical to arrange to handle
matters of tnis character outside of the usual diplomatic channels and
that the originel opinion of the War Department expressed in the secund
FILED
paragraph of letter from the Chief of Staff to the Secretary of State
dated May 10, 1935, was sound. It is. therefore recommended that no
further steps be taken towards arranging for a conference of American.
and Japanese communication experts. If this recomendation is approta

=== Page 233 === (confidence 0.94)
by you, discussion of matters concerning radio interference will be
confined in the future, as in the past, to diplomatic channels.
The Navy Department has indicated concurrence in the above
remarks and recommendations.
Sincerely yours,

The Department has received from the Federal Radio
Commission a letter dated Jamuary 4, 1934, in whioh it
is stated that within the past few months the Commission
has recsived a large mumber of reports of violations, on
the part of ship stations under the jurisdiction of
certain foreign governments, of the General Regulations
annexed to the International Radiotelegraph Convention
of Washington, 1927.
The Commission has furnished to the Department the
enolosed tabulation of the reports of violations on the
part of stations under the jurisdiction of the Japanese
Government.
It is not the desire of this Government to urge any
drastio action against the personnel involved in these
violations. The information is forwarded because it is
desirable, in order to increase the safety of life at sea,
that every effort be made to secure an adequate compliance
with the operating requirements set forth in the above
mentioned

Enclosure No. 1 to despatch
No. 851 of June 21, 1934, from
the Embassy at Tokyo.
Tokyo, February 27, 1934.
No. 203.
Excellency:
In accordance with instructions from my Government,
I have the honor to bring to Your Excellency's attention
the fact that within the past few months the American
Federal Radio Commission has received a large number of
reports of violat ons of the General Regulations annexed
to the International Ra iotelegraph Convention of Washing-
ton of 1927, on the part of ship stations under the juris-
diction of certain foreign governments. A tabulation is
enclosed of the violations by stations under the juris-
diction of the Japanese Government.
It is not the desire of my Government to urge any
drastic action gainst the personnel involved in the vio-
lations. The list of them is respectfully submitted to
Your Excellency simply for the information of the Japanese
Government, because it is desirable, in order to increase
the safety of life at sea, that ev ry effort be made to
secure an adequate compliance with the operating require-
ments set forth in the above mentioned Regulations. Article
6 of the Radiotelegraph Convention of Washington provides
that "the contracting Governments undertake to aid each
other by supplying information concerning violations of
the provisions of the present Convention and of the Regula-
tions annexed thereto".
I avail myself of this opportunity to renew to Your
Excellency the assurances of my highest consideration.
Joseph C. Grew.
His Excellency
Mr. Koki Hirota,
His Imperial Japanese Majesty's
Minister for Foreign Affairs,
etc.,

1. Station JFQ at Taihoku has been under repair to
change the frequency to 8600 kilocycles. Due to a
variety of circumstances the work was delayed, but
was completed by the end of May,last. Since then
there has been very little variation in frequency.

kilocycles was newly equipped with crystals on the
same date as the station mentioned in paragraph 1.
Since then there has been very little variation in fre-
quency.

My dear Mr. Secretary:
Departmanl of State
DEPARTMENT OF STATE
The following report from the Oommanding Officer of
the U.S.C.G. Haida at Unalaska, Alaska, is quoted for your information
with the request that representations be made to the Government of Japan
as required by Article 13, paragraph 2 of the General Radio Regulations
894.74
annexed to the International Teleconmmunication Convention of Madrid, 1932,
894.74/142
with a view to the alleviation of this interference which constitutes a
7lote
menace to the safety of life at sea:
"1. Japanese vessels cause considerable interference on 500
kilocycles, working continuously as much as an hour at a time.
They seldom use their International Calls so they may be iden-
tified, but by constant watching and noting their signal
characteristics, an association has been made with certain calls
when they were made.
2. Vessels with call letters JKTD. JIFC and JBOD are the most
constant offenders, transmitting regular traffic in Japanese code
without reference to silent periods on 500 kilocycles.
3. The interference in these cases is very bad. blanketing the
entire commercial band, making it almost impossible to copy signals
through the broad spark notes. This interference is frequently
contimuous and has caused other stations to stand by until the
Japanese stations discontimue sending of their own accord. In addi-
tion to transmitting during silent periods, they disregard requests
OCI
to stop sending or to send on frequencies other than the distress
FIL
and calling frequencies.
心
πD
10
4. It would be impossible to handle an average distress case
gi
should one occur during such time as these Japanese stations have
the air."
Very truly yours,
Eo.byher
E. O. Sykes,
Chairman.

(1) Between JYB (Toyo) 8165 KC/S and WTJ (Hawaii)
8160 KC/S and WXB (Alaska) 3170 KC/S, tlere is a space
of 5 KC/S each. Accoruingly, if the d signated frequency
in kilo cycles sho ld be maintained reciprocally, no wire-
less interfe  nce would occur. JYb of Japan is of a crys-
tal system and there is littlc or no deviation in its
transmitting frequency.
(&) JAQ (Tokyo) 8050 KC/S is of a crystal system
and a deviation in frequency is extremely less and is not
accompanied by wrong waves.
(3) The frequency of JFH (Taihoku) is 9460 KC/S. In
viev. of the fact that the difference betvecn this frequency
and the frequency of WVY (San Francisco) 3600 KC/S is very
great, it is considered that no wireless interference
would occur. If JFQ (Taihoku) is 8605 KC/S, there would
be a difference of 5 KC/S and there may occur a wireless
interference unless the two parties should maintain de-
signated frequency reciprocally. JFQ of Japan is of a
crystal system and a deviation in frequency is much less.
(4) The frequency of JYC, JYD and JYT (all of them
Tokyo) is 10,780, 13,080, and 15,760 KC/S respectively.
Because of the fact that the difference with frequency
of either WTJ or WXH of the United States is very great,
there is no fear of wireless interference being brought
about.

The statement in the penultimate paragraph of the
894.74
War Department's letter of Ootober 19, 1935, that "the
frequency of 12,240 kcs was registered by the U. S. Army
894.74/147
and was placed in actual use at Station WTJ, Fort Shafter,
T.H., prior to the date of registration and actual use
by Japan of the same frequency at Station JFB, Taihoku,"
is erroneous. According to the Bern Frequency List this
Government made a general reservation of wave length
12,240 kc on July 28, 1928. But on September 19, 1929,
the Japanese Government not only reserved this wave length,
but registered it for station JFB at Taihoku, and the War
Department only registered this wave length for their
station WTJ at Fort Shafter, Hawaii, on February 19, 1930.
In the circumstances, the Japanese station has priority,
FLED
and we can only ask the Japanese Government to help us
in this matter as a matter of courtesy. I called up
Lieutenant Pachynski of the War Department and pointed
F
out the foregoing to him. He looked the matter up and
agreed

=== Page 284 === (confidence 0.92)
DEPARTMENT OF STATE
TREATY DIVISION
-2-
agreed with me that the War Department had been mistaken
in their statement and agreed with the conclusions I
reached as stated above. In the circumstances I have
drafted the attached telegram to the Embassy at Tokyo.
Lded
td:rCdeW:mm

At present it is planned to install a reflecting apparatus on
the transmitting wire for the Taihoku (JFB) frequency which, it
is hoped, will alleviate the radio interference mentioned. The
Taihoku frequency is occasionally used for very short periods
to supplement other frequencies, but in view of the inter-
ference caused by this frequency, its use will be evoided as
much as possible, pending the installation of the reflecting
apparatus.

ica, New York, reporting a case of serious interference to the

International Telecommunication Union, Berne, Switzerland, indi-

in conflict with the dates November 29 and November 30, 1931, men-

that 8440 kilocycles is assigned to stations of the Japanese Gov-
ernment but no notification dates are shown in the three columns
reserved for that purpose.

Federal Communications Commission
Washington
D. C.
Gantlemen:
Beginning late in January l936 serious i:verference developed
to the international mobile radiocommunicati  service conducted
by our Bolinas, Calif. station KPH on 8440 ki. Prior to January
only a few infrequent cases of intermittent inerference resulted
to KPH 8440 kc operation, so that, in effect, we can say that the
operations of our station were unaffected from the time 8440 kc
was put into use at KPH on November 30, 1931.
When interference first became troublesome every effort was made
to identify the station. We know from general characteristic trans-
mitting procedure of the station and from messages exchanged with the
Japanese Telegraph Administration that the interfering station is
located somewhere in Japanese territory. It appears that the station
is conducting a fixed point to point service in a frequency band allo-
cated for exclusive mobile service by international radio regulations.
The attached Exhibit "A" covering log entries by station KPH during
the month of March 1936 presents a clear picture of the interference
resulting to mobile shore-ship service rendered by station KPH. There
has been no change in the situation since March and the conditions noted
in the exhibit are experienced today.
The attached Exhibit "B" provides for your information exchange of
radiotelegraphic communications with the Japanese Telegraph Administration
in our endeavors to establish identity of the interfering statim and clear
the interference by cooperative measures.
The attached Exhibit "C" shows high precision frequency measurements
recorded by RCA monitoring stations on KPH and the Japanese station
during the period January 1 to March 31, 1936. The freguency stability
of the stations are holding unchanged today.

=== Page 298 === (confidence 0.98)
Federal Communications Commission
-2-
June 15, 1936
A brief historical review of KPH maritime activity in the 36
meter band is as follows:
February 1928, call letters 6XI on 8490 kc, to
May 1929, when frequency changed to 8430 kc under
call letters KPH. March l930 frequency changed
to 8350 kc. July 1930 frequency changed to 8390
kc, October 1931 frequency changed to 8440 kc
in conformity with the new USA maritime frequency
allocation plan (FRC C.O. #ll9). The change to
8440 kc was mede on November 29, 1931, on which
date all affected USA coastal stations cooperatively
made a shift to new frequency assignments to fulfill
the requirements of FRC Minute #450, epproved on
October 26, 1931. Thereafter, to date, KPH has
utilized the 8440 kc channel in daily communicatbn
service with ship stations.
In consideration of the long record of uninterrupted activity
by KPH 8440 kc, we respectfully esk that representations be mede
to the Japanese Administration to the end that suitable action be
taken to clear this interference.
Yours very truly,
RADIOMARINE CORPOPATION OF AMERICA
by： Cau
Assistant Secretary

=== Page 303 === (confidence 0.99)
SS MALOLO
San Francisco, Calif.
Fet. 21, 1936
Mr. T. M. Stevens
General Superintendent
Pacific Division
Radiomarine Corporation
San Francisco, Calif.
Dear Mr. Stevens:
We have been experiencing consideralle interference for
a long time with the reception of your daily press scheoules on 8440
kilocycles, caused by a Japanese coast station, as vet unidentified
by his call letters.
This interference is very annoying and especially so on
the run between the mainland and Honolulu where at times he is very
bad and completely blankets your signals. Tt is also noticeahle
when handling regular traffic but this is only intermittently.
The Japanese station in question seems to have a very
broad signal similar in tone to WCC at Chatham and swings badly from
one side of you to the other and usually peaked on your frecuency of
8440 kilocycles. Most of the communications are in Japanese with a few
English and handled continuously during your press schedule, which
leads to the belief that it must be a Japanese point to point st tion.
Any effort on your part to elininate this interference
would be greatly appreciated by ourselves and other vessels copying
this press schedule.
Respectfully,
F. C. Dixon
SS MALOLO

=== Page 304 === (confidence 0.99)
EXTRACTS FROM SPECIAL REPORT
DATED MARCH 20, 1936
BY MANAGER OF STATTON KPH
The station causing the interference in uestion is
Japanese by observation and admission. For months the station
has been heard calling N/5 and LB9, signing B--.-- ? (B WW 2).
Our wire to you dated Feb. 27th contains thi: informatior, made
also by RCA Manila. It further stated that messages contained
data for Shanghai/Kagoshima flights, therefore possibly a Jap-
anese government station (Naval) that works in conjunction with
either Manchuoko or Japanese sections of China. Further identi-
fication by observation can bring forth no informatfon other
than that mentioned, as we have been observing this station for
a long period, likewise the RCACmeasurement station at Tnverness
has confirmed the cell letters.
Since KSE is using a 36 wave that is clear from the
Japanese QRM, why not arrange to swap waves, granting us the KSE
wave, if the Frequency Bureau is unable to move the Jap? Of
course, it would put KSE into the QRM, but KPH has too much to
lose in comparison.

=== Page 305 === (confidence 0.99)
EXTRACTS FROM SPECIAL REPORT
DATED MARCH 21, 1936
BY MANAGER OF STATION KPH
Went on 20 KW at 0800 GMT March 21st, on 8440 kc.
Press transmission was not jammed by the Japanese interference
until 10:00 GMT, when he came in and tuned up his transmitter
shutting it off again until 10:18 GMT. It then took him until
10:30 GMT to get it retuned. Press was finished at 10:14 CMT.
At 10:30 this station called LB9 numerous times,
signed B-MW-2, or B --.-- 2, and went on traffic. Broke several
times and made V's. At 1l:15 GMT he quit for the night, but
despite the fact that our 20 KW ran continuously during this
period, the Japanese station was able to apparently deliver his
traffic.
The fact that the station did not come back after 1l:15
GWT was not due to our QPM, but to the fact that it is some
Japanese holiday. (So informed hy MTTSUBISHI ship).
Estimate the Jap to be using at least 40 KW. Added to
that he probably works to the China coast, 750 miles at the most.
That means, to jam the receiving station, we have to cover some
5500 miles, and deliver more signal than he does ... which is
impossible.
Identification of the station, further than reported
cannot be made, as the Japanese station sends in Japanese entirely
and the "Japanese" refuse to identify it.
In the meanwhile, traffic will not be seriously jeopardized
as long as 47 is available, but press certainly will be jammed.

CONFIDENTIAL

My dear Mr. Secretary:
153
Reference is made to previous correspondence, and especially to
your confidential letter of October 13, 1936, inclosing a copy of a
despatch from the American Embassy at Tokyo, which transmits a trans-
894.74/154
lation of a memorandum received fron the Japanese Foreign Office, all
concerning the interference experienced by the Radio Corporation of
America Station KPH, Bolinas, California, operating on 8440 kc, caused
by the transmissions from a Japanese Government station operating on
the same frequency.
This matter has been taken up with the Radio Corporation of
America with the thought in mind that the frequency of KPH, Bolinas,
might be changed to some other portion of the 8ooo kc band. However,
because of the large number of stations occupying this particular band,
it has been impossible, up to the present, to decide upon a suitable
alternate frequency, and therefore a satisfactory solution of the
case is still pending further study. Inasmuch as this complaint of
interference was raised by the United States, and since the Japanese
Right
Government has not complained of interference, it is assumed that this
delay will create no hardship on the service now being carried on by
the Japanese stations.
The Commission would appreciate being informed of the nature of
the reply received from the Bureau of the International Telecommunica-
tion Union at Bern, Switzerland, in view of the circumstances associated
with the notification of this frequency to the Bureau.
FILA
The Commission appreciates your cooperation and the action taken
in this matter.

October 26, 1936
Berne, le 23 octobre 1936.
Monsieur le Ministre,
Par votre lettre du 14 octobre 1936 vous avez bien voulu
nous donner connaissance d'un télégramme du Département d'
Etat à Washington qui signale l'omission de la date de noti-
fication en regard de certaines fréquences publiées dans la
Liste des fréquences.
A ce sujet, nous avons l'honneur de vous donner les
renseignements suivants:
Il arrive qu'un Office veuille faire connaftre aux
administrations de l'Unior qu'il se propose d'utiliser telle
fréquence. Cette communication ne donne aucune autre pré-
cision. Dans ce cas, nous publions cette fréquence dans la
liste, mais sans date de notification, car le Règlement
général de Madrid prescrit à l'appendice 6, sous "Liste
des fréquences", II a) ce qui suit:
"La date de notification d'une fréquence, à insérer
dans la colonne 3a est la date que porte la comrrunication
par laquelle le B.U. a été informé de la première attribu-
tion de cette fréquence à une station du pays indiqué. Le
nom de cette station figure dans la colonne 5."
Cette disposition, à notre avis, fait dépendre la
date de notification (colonne 3a) de l'existence d'un nom
de station dans la colonne 5.
Plus tard, lors de l'attribution de cette fréquence
à une station déterminée, nous ajoutons le nom de cette
station dans la liste des fréquences et nous indiquons, comme
date de notification, la date à laquelle l'Office intéressé
nous a fait connaftre le nom de la station qui utilise la
fréquence.
Toutefois, étant donné que, sous le régime du Règlement
général de Washington, la date de notification d'une fré-
quence ne dépendait pas de l'indication d'un nom de station,
nous faisons une exception pour les fréquences qui nous ont
été communiquées avant l'entrée en vigueur du Réglement
général de Madrid.
Exemple: La fréquence de 8440 kc-s, qui vous intéresse tout
particulièrement, nous a été notifiée par le Japon le 9 nov-
embre l928 sans indication du nom de la station qui l'utilise.
Si, pour cette fréquence, le Japon nous notifiait le nom d'une
station le ler décembre i936, nous indiquerions, comme date
de notification, dans la colonne 3a de la liste, le 9 novembre
1928 et non le ler décembre 1936.
S'il s'agissait d'une attribution à une station mobile,
nous la publierions sous la forme indiquée à l'appendice 6
au Règlement général de Madrid (I. Généralités, b), c'est-
à-dire, sans aucune date de notification dans la colonne 3a.
Nous ajoutons que, lors de l'entrée en vigueur du Règle-
ment général de Madrid, nous avons biffé la date de notifi-
cation qui figurait dans la liste pour chaque fréquence non
suivie du nom d'une station. Mais cette même date sera
reproduite dans la liste dès que l'Office intéressé nous
A la Légation des Etats-Unis d'Amérique,
4, Bernastrasse,
BERNE.
aura

aura indiqué le nom de la station qui l'utilise.
Veuillez agréer, Monsieur le Ministre, l'assurance
de notre haute considération.

Enclosure to Despatch No. 4644
October 26, 1936
AU DE L'UNION INTERNATIONALE
DES TELECOMMUNICATIONS
No. R 7-403
Bern, 0ctober 23, 1936.
Mr. Minister:
In your letter of October 14, l936, you were good enough
to communicate to us the text of a telegram from the Depart-
ment of State at Washington which points out the omission
of the date of notification as regards certain frequencies
published in the List of Frequencies.
In this connection, we have the honor to give the
following information:
It happens that an Office desires to advise adminis-
trations of the Union that it proposes to utilize a certain
frequency. The communication, however, sets forth no
further details. In such a case, we publish this frequency
In the list, but without a notification date, inasmuch as
the General Regulations of Madrid stipulate in appendix 6,
under "List of Frequencies", II (a), the following:
"The date of notification of a frequency, to be
included in column 3a, is the date appearing on the
communication by which the B.U. was informed of the
first allocation of this frequency to a station of the
country indicated. The name of this station appears
In column 5.
This provision, in our cpinion, makes the date of noti-
fication (column 3a) dependent upon the existence of a
station name in column 5.
Later, at the time of the allocation of this frequency
to a given station, we include the name of this station in
the list of frequencies and we indicate as the date of noti-
fication the date on which the Office concerned informed us
of the name of the station which utilizes the frequency.
However, inasmuch as under the system of the General
Regulations of Washington, the date of notification of a
frequency was not dependent upon the indication of a station
name, we make an exception as regards frequencies communi-
cated to us before the entry into force of the General Reg-
ulations of Madrid.
Example: Frequency 8440 kilocycles, which 1s of
specific interest to you, was notified to us by Japan
on November 9. l928. without an indication of the name
of the station which utilizes it. If, for this fre-
quency, Japan should notify us of the name of a station
on December 1, 1936, we would indicate as the date of
notification, in column 3a of the list, November 9,
1928, and not December 1, 1936.
If it were a question of an allocation to a mobile
station, we would publish it according to the manner
7
set forth in appendix 6 to the General Regulations of
Madrid (I. Generalities, b), that is to say, without
any date of notification in column 3a.
We add that, upon the entry into force of the General
Regulations of Madrid, we struck out the date of notifica-
To the Legation of the United
tion
States of America,
Bern.

=== Page 331 === (confidence 0.98)
- 2 -
tion appearing in the list for every frequency not followed
by the name of a station. But this same date will be re-
produced in the list as soon as the Office concerned shall
have advised us of the name of the station which utilizes
it.
Please accept, Mr. Minister, the assurance of our high
consideration.
BUREAU OF INTERNATIONAL TELECOMMUN-
ICATIONS UNION
The Vice-Director:
SCHWILL.
