September 2, 1938.
In order to obviate any awkwardness in complaining
of interference by a Japanese station in Manchukuo, refer-
ences to the places in which the two offending Japanese
stations are situated have been omitted. The stations can
be sufficiently identified by their call letters and fre-
894.74/167
894.74
quencies. They are both listed as Japanese-operated sta-
tions in the list of frequencies published by the Bern
Bureau of International Telecommunication Union. Station
JMO-4 1s l1sted as being operated by the Manchuria Tele-
phone and Telegraph Company, which company, in turn, is
listed under Japan in the list of frequencies.

Press Wireless, Inc. hes advised of seri us interference
to the transmissions of St:tion WDD, Hicksville, New York
on 7510 kc occasioned by Japanese St&tion JVP, Tokyo, Japen
operatin: on the same frequency.
894.74/174
This frequency is in the band allocated to fixed service
by international agreement. It is reported that Station JVP
is using this frequency for broadcasting which does not appear
to be in accordance with the recommendations of the recent
International Telecommunication Conferences. It is noted that
in accordance with the list of frecuencies issued by the Bureau
of the International Telecommunications Union, Station JVP is
listed as in the fixed point-to-point service to Europe, and hes
been in operation since 1934 without occasioning perticuler inter-
ference difficulties to Station WDD, therefore, it would ppear
that recent changes in the activities of this station have
occasioned these difficulties.
It should be noted in this connection that the United Ststes
has priority for this frequency over Japan in that it was notified
on March 23, 1932 as against the Japanese notification date of
September 24, 1932.
The most recent cases of interference caused by Station JVP
have been recorded as follows:
November 15, 1245 to 1325 GMT

=== Page 382 === (confidence 0.99)
1. Regarding the interference between the Japanese
station JDK, 8,720 kilocycles and KJY, 17,440 kilocycles,
while it is believed that uch interference may be caused
by the second harmonic or the Japanese station, the carry-
ing intensity of tnis har.onic in com urison with the carry-
ing intensity of the basic wave has been reduced to, at the
most - 37 decibels. Therefore, with such very small elec-
trical power, it is not believed that any interference is
given to American stations.
2. Regarding the interference between the Japanese
station JM0 - 4, 15,905 kilocycles and WRK, 15,910 kilo-
cycles, an investigation of the broaccasting irequency of
the Japanese station indicates that broadcasting at 15,905.1
kilocycles, is for all practical purposes maintaining the
officially e stablished frequency. In view of the fact
that there is a difference of five kilocycles in the fre-
quencies of both stations, if there is further interfer-
ence, it is believed to be caused by the variation of the
broadcasting frequency of station WRk.

894.74/177
I have the honor to transmit herewith an original
signed communication in which the Kobe Central Telegraph
Office requests an American company in Kobe to make use of
the Japanese Government wireless service for the despatch
of telegrams abroad instead of submarine routes.
The Japanese telegraph office expresses regret that
the use of submarine routes for a large number of messages
FILED
results in overseas payments of nearly 10,000,000 yen yearly
with a very adverse effect upon Japan's international
accounts.
The telegraph office adds that the use of the wireless
service not only will help Japan's international accounts
but also will contribute towards lowering telegraph rates
through the reduction of exchange loss calculations in inter-
national settlement of accounts.
F/FG

KOBE CENTRAL TELEGRAPH OFFICE.
7th November,
1938
KOBE,
)
Mr. E. L. Matteson,
Messrs. Dollar Steamship Lines Inc.
7 Kaigan-dori,
Kobe-ku,
K O B E.
Dear Sir,
We extend our hearty thanks to you for your kind co-operation
in the use of wireless service (Anten) for the dispatch of telegrams
abroad.
It is regrettable, however, that a large number of messages are
still being sent over the submarine routes, such as Fil, noreast,
etc., with the result that the overseas payments of the cable charges
amount to a large sum of nearly 10,000,000 Yen annually.
This heavy payment naturally has a very adverse effect upon the
balance of the country's international accounts, a fact which is
particularly regrettable when it is remembered that, in view of the
present national situation.
The Government, with the full co-operation of the public, is
taking every means possible to minimize the overseas payments to
stabilize the national financial position.

=== Page 389 === (confidence 0.99)
K O B E .
Under these circumstances, we sincerely solicit your sympa-
thetic co-operation, and ask you to specify the "Anten" wireless route
in dispatching your telegrams in future.
More than ten years have elapsed since the inauguration of our
wireless telegraph service and with the amazing progress made in wire-
less science, the service has been greatly improved year after year.
With direct circuits to more than 2o important countries th-
roughout the world, the service can be fu. ly relied upon for speed and
accuracy.
Furthermore, we should like to draw your attention to the fact
that the use of the wireless service will not only improve the state of
international accounts, but will also contribute towards the lowering
of telegraph rates, through the reduction of the exchange loss usually
calculated in the international settlement of accounts.
Since we are ever desirous of improving the general service,
your kind suggestions or enquiries will be greatly appreciated.
Thanking you in advance for your further co-operation.
We remain.
Yours faithfully,
Kobe Central Telegraph Office,
Superintendent.

894.74/180
Referring to my letter of February 27, 1939, and in
further reply to your letter of February 20, 1939, File
IN 894.74/178, Press Wireless, Inc., has advised that the
transmissions of Station WDD, Hicksville, are not now
being interfered with by Japanese Station JVP operating on
7510 kc.
The last instance of this interference occurred between
4:15 a. m. and 5:50 a. m., P.S.T., on February 7, 1939. During
this period, the interference was of such intensity that no
traffic could be received at San Francisco from the New York
transmissions on Station WDD. It appears, therefore, that the
FULED
Japanese station has either discontinued broadcasting on this
frequency or has shifted the period of operation to some other
time of day to avoid conflicting with Station WDD.
Your cooperation in this matter is appreciated.

My dear Mr. Secretary:
This is with reference to your letter of December 28, 1938,
File No. IN 894.74/175, with which you transmitted a copy of a
despatch from the American Embassy at Tokyo relative to interfer-
ence caused by Japanese stations to Press Wireless, Inc. stations.
This despatch stated that the Japanese authorities felt that the
interference caused to Station KJY operating on 17440 kc by the
second harmonic of Station JDK, operating on 8720 kc had been
eliminated.
The Commission is informed, however, by Press Wireless,
Incorporated that this interference continues serious and is a
daily occurence disrupting the transmission of traffic by Station
KJY.
It will accordingly be appreciated if you will again bring
this matter to the attention of the appropriate authorities with
the request that the interference caused by the second harmonic
of Station JDK be eliminated.
Sincerely yours,
C

July 7, 1939
Commander F. W. Connor, USN
Room 349A
Federal Office Building
Civic Center
San Francisco, California
Dear Sir:
Please forgive my delay in sending you the enclosed letters reporting
deliberate Japanese interference with broadcasts from General Electric
international Station W6xBE. We are just now beginning to get caught
up with the deluge of mail on the station. You will be interested to
know that within the last few days we have had many American visitors
who have just returned to this country from China and several of them
have reported in detail on deliberate Japanese interference with our
news broadcasts in English. They are apparently using a noise machine
such as referred to in some of the enclosed letters. I judge from the
various reports, both written and verbal, that on some days they
attempt to interfere only with our news broadcasts in English, but on
other days they try to spoil our entire program of music and speech.
A Mr. J. C. Oliver of Shanghai, who came in to see us with his family,
today, reported that as far as he could tell, the Japanese were doing
their utmost to interfere with our Oriental beam broadoasts from 4:00
to 9:00 a.m. Pacific Standard Time - 8:00 to 1:00 a.m. Shanghai time,
but that apparently the Japanese have not gotten wise to the fact that
our Latin American broadoasts from 3:30 to 8:15 p.m. Pacific Standard
Time are being received fairly well in Shanghai, and so many listeners
there are getting the English news which we put on our Latin American
beam. For your confidential information, some time ago we made our
Latin American beam bi-directional instead of uni-directional, so that
the programs in reality are not only beamed to Latin America, but are
also beamed to the Orient. When the attached letters have served your
purpose, please return them to me.
Sineerely yours,
arris
Manager
ETBHarris:ed
Station W6XBE

Commander P. W. Connor, USN

San Francisco, California
Dear Sir:
Please forgive my delay in sending you the enclosed letters reporting
deliberate Jxpanese interference with broadcasts from General Electric
international Station W6XBE. We are just now beginning to get caught
up with the deluge of mail on the station. You will be interested to
know that within the last few days we have had many American visitors
who have just returned to this country from China and several of them
have reported in detail on deliberate Japanese interference with our
news broadcasts in English. They are apparently using a noise machine
such as referred to in some of the enclosed letters. I judge from the
various reports, both written and verbal, that on some days they
attempt to interfere only with our news. broedcasts in English, but on
other days they try to spoil our entire program of music and speech.
A Mr. J. C. Oliver. of Shanghai, who came in to see us with his family,
today, reported that as far as he could tell, the Japanese were doing
their utmost to interfere with our Oriental beam broadoasts from 4:00
to 9:00 a.m. Pacific Standard Time - 8:00 to 1:00 a.m. Shangha1 time,
but that apparently the Japanese have not gotten wise to the fact that
our Latin American broadoasts from 3:30 to 8:15 p.m. Pacific Standard
Time are being received fairly well in Shanghai, and so many listeners
there are getting the English news which we put on our Latin American
beam. For your confidential informatión, some time ago we made our
Latin American beam bi-directional instead of uni-directional, so that
the programs in reality are not only beamed to Latin Amerios, but are
also beamed to the Orient. When the attached letters have served your
purpose, please return them to me.
sineerely yours,
mt
arris
Manager
ETBHarris:ed
Station W6XBE

My Dear Sir:
Last evening April 15th, 10 p.m. here, and 6 a.m.
there, we accidentally tuned in on your station; it was
reat joy to us here, in Cheungchau, Hongkong, Asia.
I have been tryin to get something from America for
several ye rs, and this is the first time. Occasi nally,
others sey, that they pick up America but feintly. I an
an American so naturally I am poud t hear something
direct. from there.
心
Reception was excellent, A-1 and could not have been
any better. N.B. I have an eight tube Philco Radio, with an
Aerial wire only eight feet long, which is connected to the
frame of a steel window in my room, no aerial wire, on the
outisde. This is beyond my incomprehension.
Also, the news came through with force, tho there vas
a little interference. I think some one was trying to cut
off the news as the interference started in when something
was said re the Japs, Berlin and Italy. "ews from Munila
is often interfered with.
Yours sincerely,
Rev. W. M. Burnside.

. Saugstad:
FE concurs in the opinions expressed
in the attached memorandum of July 27,
1939, in regard to apparently deliberate
Japanese interference in the Far East
with broadcasts from the General Electric
International Station W6XBE. It is as-
sumed that the reference in the second
paragraph of the memorandum to the "second
paragraph of the Tokyo telegram" is a
reference to the first substantive
paragraph of that telegram.
It is brought to the attention of
FP that one of the letters enclosed
with the letter of July 18, 1939, from
the Navy Department was written to the
above-mentioned station by Mrs. Frank S.
Williams, wife of the American Commercial
Attache at Tokyo. FE is of the opinion
that certain remarks made by Mrs. Williams
(those sentences which are underlined)
in the letter ought not to have been
made. It is suggested that FP may wish
to draft an instruotion to the Embassy
at Tokyo in that sense.

STRIOTLY CONFIDENTIAL
My dear Gommander Wixon:
Referring to your confidential memorandum of
July 18, 1939 in regard to alleged Japanese interference
894.74/183
with United States news broadoasts to the Far East, I now
inform you of the receipt of a telegram from the Ameriean
Embassy at Tokyo, reporting that so far as radio reception
in Japan is concerned licenses for shortwave receiving
sets capable of receiving United States broadeasts are
granted only to members of the staff of embassies, lega-
tions and consulates, and that no report of interference
894.
has been obtained from those persons.
It is added that reports of Japanese interference
in Ohina or elsewhere in the Far East require substantial
evidence of their authenticity before any protest is made
to the Government of Japan.

A-M Mr.Warren.
The attached telegram from the Embassy at Tokyo re-
fers to a telegram sent by the Department in regard to
apparently deliberate Japanese interference in the Far
East with broadcasts from the General Eleotric Interna-
tional Station W6XBE. Our telegram requested the Embassy
894.74/184
to bring to the attention of the appropriate Japanese
authorities so much of it as the Embassy deemed advisable.
It is believed that the contents of the second para-
graph of the Tokyo telegram may be brought to the notice
of the Navy Department, and it is assumed that this will
be done informally by Mr. Warren. If a formal letter is
desired, this Division will be glad to prepare it.
This Division questions whether the contents of the
last paragraph of the Tokyo telegram should be given to
the Navy Department, but in any event it is believed that
that Department should be informed that if it can obtain
F/A
more tangible proof of deliberate Japanese interference,
such
072794

Mechanical interference referred to in the Department's

General Electric Company regardiny this matter.

ference has now almost ceased. Thc source is not known,

inquiries and shall inform the Department if further in-
formation becomes available.

Dear Mr. Grew:

of Mrs. Frank williams! letter of April 1, 1939, to a

894.74
Office of Naval Intelligence) which brought to our at-
tention a copy of Mrs. Williams' letter. I am therefore
writing to suggest that, in speaking to Mr. Williams of
his wife's letter, you make olear that Mrs. williams is
under no ciroumstanoes to write to the radio station in
question in regard to the matter. As the radio station
supplied a copy of the letter to the Office of Naval
Intelligence in striot confidence, it might be embarrass-
ing to that office were the radio station to be led to
infer through a communication from Mrs. williams that a
copy of the letter had been made available to the
Department.
with best wishes,
